So the reply says the command was received, not that it had an effect, and only the poll separates the two. That is the same criterion the lights and heating settled on, now shown to be necessary on gas rather than merely prudent | Support; it completes the gas closing direction with a real transition | One frame, phase seven, through the gate. The valve was opened by hand before the send and opened again after, and it is the kitchen's only. Opening stays refused everywhere. No push, Home Assistant update, Ingress mutation or add-on capture occurred | One send in each direction. Nothing is known about a valve left shut for longer, and the opening direction remains unavailable by design. Whether some other device also reports or reacts to the gas state was not looked for beyond `0x2A` |
| M4-E147 | The `0x2A` reply carries two devices' states, not one, and this project's earlier reading of its trailing bytes as fixed was wrong | 268 `0x2A` replies across four runs, every one of whose byte 11 equals the `0x1B` state at that moment, moving in both directions with it, 2026-08-30 | Noticed because byte 11 changed from `03` to `04` in the frames printed while checking the gas transition | The frame is `F7 0E 01 2A 04 40 10 00 19 <light batch-off> 1B <gas> <XOR> EE`: byte 8 is the light device's address and byte 10 the gas device's, each followed by that device's state. `M4-E134` recorded `19`, `1B` and `03` as bytes that never moved, and that was an artefact of the valve staying shut for the whole `batchoff` run, not a property of the frame; the row now says so. Following the gas takes this device 1,470 to 1,758 ms, the size of its own polling period. **This is why the legacy calls it a multi function switch**, and it fits an entrance panel carrying batch-off and gas together | Correction to `M4-E134`, and it explains the legacy's naming | Observation only; no frame was sent to `0x2A` in this run | **The `1B` pair has no tested set frame.** The legacy builds only the `19` variant, gas is already controllable through `0x1B` directly, and deriving a `1B` variant would be an invention; it was not tried. Whether a third pair exists for some device not installed here is unknown, since the frame's length is fixed at fourteen bytes and both pairs are accounted for. The correlation is 268 samples but from one household on one day |
| M4-E148 | The wallpad does not call the elevator on this line, and this line does not report a call made from the hallway either, though the car demonstrably moved for one; `M4-E150` later showed the line will still carry a call we inject | Run `elev-a`, 145 s of hallway-button silence then two wallpad-originated calls; across 44,986 frames `0x34` is `kind=01` 4,224 times and `kind=02` and `kind=04` zero times, 2026-08-30 | Stage A of `.agent/plan-elevator-measurement.md`. The operator first pressed the hallway button, which produced nothing, then asked whether it had to be the wallpad — and it did | Pressing the hallway call button changed nothing anywhere on the line: 89 elevator frames all idle and twelve distinct frames in the whole window, every one routine. **That call worked** — the operator says it brought the car to this floor, which is why the wallpad call two minutes later finished in two seconds. So the elevator was moving while this line stayed silent, and the reading is stronger than a bare negative: the line does not report calls raised from the hallway. Pressing the wallpad's own down button changed the state frame within two seconds. A byte-level sweep of the three seconds around that moment shows only routine polling; **no command left the wallpad on this line at all**. That matches the legacy's own annotation that this frame runs wallpad to hallway mini-pad. This says the wallpad does not call over this line. **It does not say the line cannot carry a call**, and the operator reports that the legacy add-on's elevator call did work here, which is direct evidence that it can. The absence is the wallpad's choice of path, not the frame's viability. The legacy's `packet_call_type` defaults to 0 and so does the add-on's setting, so the frame that worked is variant 0, the same skeleton the current add-on builds | Correction; it removes the observational footing the elevator call plan assumed and makes reading the whole of what this line offers | Observation only. Nothing was sent to `0x34` and the phase list was not widened | Sending the variants was not tried here, and the operator's report that the legacy's call worked is testimony rather than a measurement in this session: which variant, and on which firmware, is not pinned down by it. Two wallpad calls, both with the car already at this floor, so no long travel was watched from a call we could time from its start. What path the wallpad actually uses is outside this line and outside this tool |
| M4-E149 | All four elevator states are watched live and the legacy's reading of the high nibble is falsified: it is the direction the car is going or about to go, not the direction it is moving | Same run: `06` at 06:26:10 and `b6` at 06:26:49, both at floor `04` with car `0b`, against one elevator in the building | The gap was visible in `M4-E134`-era work: the capture on disk held only an up call, so `5` and `A` were observed and `6` and `B` were not | State `06` is stopped with a down call pending and `b6` is descending with one; both resolve to `01` arrived and then to `00 00 00` within two seconds. Floor reads `04` while active and `00` when idle, so the field is the car's position and not the call's origin. **The car field is always `0b` and the building has one elevator**, which the operator confirmed, so the legacy's reading of that byte as `ev_dev_idx`, an nth-car index, cannot hold here: there is no eleventh car. It is not a meaningless constant either, since it drops to `00` when idle, and it stays `0b` throughout a travel. A second call from floor 1 gave the whole journey: `06` at floor 1, `a6` rising, floor jumping 1 to 3 to 4 because the car outruns the 1.3 to 2.0 s frame period, then `b6` held for eight seconds while the car sat at floor 4 with its doors open, then `01` and idle. **That eight seconds is why the legacy's reading of `B` as descending is wrong**: nothing was descending. A third call, up this time, settles it. Held at floor 4 in the same place in the sequence, a down call reads `b6` for eight seconds and an up call reads `a5` for seven — same physical state, different value, so the nibble tracks the call and not the motion. It is the direction the car is going **or about to go**, which also explains `a5` sitting at floor 1 for 21 seconds without the floor changing. **It never falls to `0` while a call is live**, so a state machine must not read stopped from this nibble; the only real stops are `00` idle and `01` arrived. The `05`/`06` phase, registered but not yet moving, is real and lasted 3.4 s here against one frame in the down call. Registration to arrival was 14.8 s, 15.0 s and 37.0 s across the three calls, so a timeout must be sized for the worst; arrival to idle was 1.9 s in all three | Support for the state decode, and it retires one reading of the car field without replacing it | Observation only, across three runs | **What the car byte means is unknown.** A building with two or more cars would discriminate and this one cannot. Basement encoding is untested, since no call went below the lobby. Both calls had the car already at floor 4, so the floor field was watched changing only in the earlier capture, not here |

The car was at floor 7: it descended to 5 as `b5`, reached floor 4 as `a5`, held eight seconds, then `01` and idle. **That is the discriminating case for the high nibble** — a car above the calling floor answering an up call — and it settles `M4-E149`: the nibble is the direction the car is going *or about to go*, actual motion while travelling and service direction while stopped at the destination. The legacy's "moving upside/downside" is right only for the travelling half | Support; it opens elevator calling and closes the high-nibble question | Five frames, all on the phase-eight list, all through the gate. A car was brought to this floor at 18:34 on a shared building facility, with the operator's approval and knowing the neighbours would see it | **The revoke was not tested.** It went out at 18:35:21, eight seconds after the call had already completed and returned to idle, and the tool's own `matchingFrameAgoMs` of 1,550 ms marks its apparent reply as a false positive. Whether a call can be cancelled is still unknown. **The first round's "no response" verdicts were partly my own mask's doing.** `--expect f70d01340141100006` demands the state byte be exactly `06`, and a registering call skips that: `a6` if the car is already moving, `01` if it is already on this floor. A prefix mask cannot ask for "low nibble 6". Re-judged from the state stream, variant 1 registered on three of the four sends that can be judged — the fourth had a call already live — with latencies of 1,582, 1,588 and 1,838 ms, and only 18:33:31 genuinely drew nothing. So **variant 1 works in both directions** and this row's earlier claim to the contrary is withdrawn. Variant 0 drew nothing on either of its two sends, against a legacy default of 0, which is a real contrast on thin numbers. A later call caught the car in the basement and the floor field read **`b1`**, confirming the legacy's string encoding for basements and closing that gap; that journey took 39.2 s. **The revoke has no function here at all**: the operator says the wallpad offers no way to cancel a call, so the legacy's `makePacketRevokeCall` does not apply to this estate. Our three sends drew no reaction, which fits, though the two grounds differ in kind — ours is three silences, theirs is knowing the equipment. Neither says the frame is meaningless on another estate. It was originally recorded here as untested for want of a window: With the car parked on this floor a call resolves inside two seconds and leaves no gap to cancel in, and we cannot arrange for it to be far away. Stopped there rather than keep calling a shared lift |
| M4-E151 | The wallpad's 5 to 40 °C range holds in all four zones, the device enforces the bottom itself, and a rejected value is still echoed by the direct reply | Run `heat-range`, 1,051 frames with zero unparsed bytes, seventeen armed sends answered in 99 to 115 ms, sixteen confirmed by the poll, 2026-08-30 | The operator asked for the range to be measured per zone and raised the tool's ceiling from 23 to 40 to allow it | 5 and 40 were applied in every zone and 23 restored each time. **4 °C was not applied**: the direct reply echoed `04` and the poll's target stayed at 23. The write was not ignored though — 2.2 s later the zone's state went from `04` to `01` with the target unchanged, so the command is **partly applied**, taking the enable and discarding the value. That makes this the sharpest case yet for judging by the poll: gas showed a reply that was merely identical in both outcomes, and here the reply carries a number the device refused to adopt. An implementation trusting the reply would report a 4 °C target that does not exist. The 34 values between the ends were left unmeasured on purpose, being the same frame with a different byte at the cost of gas | Support; it fixes the settable range at 5 to 40 with the bottom edge measured rather than assumed | Every send was on the phase-nine list and through the gate. **40 °C in rooms at 24 to 27 is real demand**, and writing a target switches its zone on, so each zone burned for about three seconds — one poll period — before 23 took it back. The operator asked for this knowing it. The final poll matches the baseline: four zones off, targets 23 | The top edge is not measured the way the bottom is: 41 is refused by the tool, so whether the device would also reject it is unknown by choice. Nothing says the partial application seen at 4 °C is what happens at 41. One send per value per zone. The three seconds at 40 were long enough for the poll and are not a measurement of what the boiler did |
| M4-E152 | The doorbell does not exist on this bus: the operator rang it, the wallpad streamed the door camera, and the line carried the same twelve frames it had carried for the previous half minute | Run `entrance-a`, 203 s and 1,092 reads, twelve distinct frames before the bell and the same twelve after, 2026-08-30 | Stage A of `.agent/plan-entrance-measurement.md`, run as a prediction rather than a survey: the capture's door-open moment had already shown nothing moving, and this was the test of that | `0x1E` carries two frames in 44,986: a query seen 4,865 times, every one byte-identical, and the door-open seen three. There is no field in the query for a state and no separate reply, so nothing about a call could be carried even in principle. The video stream reaching the wallpad while the line stayed unchanged puts the bell, the call and the picture all on some other path. **The add-on therefore cannot detect a doorbell at all** — not the arrival, not the call being answered. Anything it shows for a household entrance call can only rest on the `0x1E 02` frame, which arrives when the door is already being opened: the end of the exchange, not its start | Correction; the specification's entrance section describes monitoring defects that are moot, since the input those defects mishandle does not exist here | Observation only. Nothing was sent, the refusal on `0x1E 02` was not opened, and the operator rang their own bell. A second trial went further: out of the flat, door shut, bell pressed, back in by hand, video checked on the wallpad, then **the wallpad's own door-open button pressed**. Two frames were new in 128 s — the door-open three times, and one heating poll whose zone 2 reading had risen a degree. The bell again produced nothing, independently of the first trial. **The door-open button emits three frames 0.69 s apart**, 1.38 s end to end, matching the capture's spacing, and they take the `0x1E` query's slot in the polling rotation rather than adding to it. Nothing replies, and no other device moves. That fixes the frame's identity inside our own run against a known press, where the capture could only show it after the fact | This is one bell press in one household. It does not say what a communal-entrance call looks like, which is out of this scope and may use another device or the subphone line. It also does not say the frame is absent from every estate — only that it is absent here, where `0x7F` has never appeared either |

## Stop rules

- Stop on any secret-like file, unexpected tracked/dirty legacy change, path collision, unsigned-commit failure, or unexplained worktree mutation.
- Never bypass signing, hook trust, a failed exact-model resolution, or a repeated P0/P1 finding.
- A hook may emit only fixed allowlisted bootstrap text. It must not inject transcripts, environment variables, web/repository text, or this ledger's contents.
- Treat the ignored control-plane Graphify output created by the global Git hook as non-product evidence. Do not manually create a product CodeGraph index or semantic Graphify labels before product TypeScript exists.
- M2 and M3.0–M3.3 plus M4.2–M4.4 are complete within their recorded scopes.
  M4-E80 through M4-E89 complete only the M4.5 native/static light-observation
  repair in a signed local commit. Any signature/base/sentinel mismatch,
  unexpected path, or need for a live or external action stops the work. Home
  Assistant/sidebar/Ingress, Capture, private-LAN/EW11, actual TX/control,
  device change, package installation, local Docker, push/force push, and
  release each require fresh explicit authorization.

## Resume procedure

1. Confirm the active task and hard boundaries in this file.
2. Run the bootstrap contract in order, recording claim-level evidence or justified N/A.
3. Inspect `git status --short --branch` and the last signed commit.
4. Treat M2, M3.0–M3.3 and M4.2–M4.11 as complete within their recorded bounds.
   **Anything that moves one of the positions below rewrites this step.** That is
   the commit that moves HEAD, in the same commit, and the push that moves
   `origin/main`, in the work unit that records the publication. A stale value here
   is a contract violation and not a mere inaccuracy, because a resumed session that
   follows it verifies the wrong thing and reports a failure that is not there. That
   is exactly what happened between M4.5 and `0.3.0`. A containing commit cannot
   record its own SHA, so HEAD is identified by subject and every other position by
   SHA.

   | Position | Expected |
   | --- | --- |
   | HEAD subject | `docs(m4): repair the stale resume procedure and changelog placement` |
   | HEAD parent | `ed0b97836855663fef4d8e5e764f433b2dda8616` · `docs(m4): plan a local tool that sees a command and its reply together` |
   | `origin/main` | `2bab75e544a833f3dd681ac553c093326e88e969` · `docs(m4): record the 0.2.8 publication` |

   All three must carry a Good signature. Expect a clean worktree, empty staging,
   and local `main` exactly three signed commits ahead of `origin/main`. Only
   `origin/main` must equal `git ls-remote` and public GitHub `main`; all three
   local commits ahead of it are unpublished.
5. No evidence row in this file authorizes publication or live validation. Do not
   infer authority for push, Home Assistant update, browser/Ingress,
   socket/EW11/private LAN, Capture, actual TX/device, package, Docker, force push,
   or release. Obtain a fresh explicit user instruction before any such action.
   `M4-E122` plans a local measurement tool whose Epic E4 writes real frames to the
   RS485 bus; the user authorized Epics E0 through E3, which need no hardware, and
   E4 is gated on its own separate instruction.
